APR 6-1940
Dear Mr Secretary:
OFFICE OF THE SECRETARY
894.24/696
In e recent letter to a constituent,
894.24/896
Congressman Bruce Berton of New York made the following
statements about the proposed embargo on the export of
war materials to Japan:
"Neither Japan nor China has declared war, nor have
they withdrawn their ambassadors, the reason being
presumebly that each thinks it is profiting more
from shipments from this country than it would profit
if war were declared which would automatically cause
696
our Neutrality Act to be invoked.
P 1940
BECELVVD
DIAISION OF
"An embargo, of course, is the nost serious step a
nation can take short of war and in many, if not most,
cases has been followed by war. I have great confi-
dence in Secretary Hull and have felt that in this
vital matter Congress ought not to make a move until
the State Department, having as it does all the facts,
comes to us with a recommendation."
Mr Barton seems under a misapprehension
in regard to the maintaining of diplomatic relations
G/A
between Japan and China, and also as to the idea that
embargoes are frequently followed by war. When Mr Castle
made this statement he was asked for instances and was
unable to mention a single one.
194

judgment.

Mr. Basil Harris, Special Assistant to the Secretary
of the Treasury, called me by telephone this afternoon.
894.24/89
He said that the Secretary of the Treasury had asked him
to call to inform me that the Treasury had been informed
by the Collector of Customs in New York that A. S. Gauaine,
of the General Aircraft Company, 527 Fifth Avenue, New
8
York, was offering 20,000 tons of molybdenum for sale,
FILED
and that Iwa1 and Company, a Japanese concern, was
apparently about to purchase this molybdenum.
I told Mr. Harris that I would look into the matter.
X
G/A
Joseph C. Green

Apr11 16, 1940.
APR19 1940
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Upon the receipt of the information transmitted to
844.24
Mr. Green by Mr. Basil Harris in regard to an alleged
offer of molybdenum to a Japanese concern by A. S.
Gaudine, of the General A1rcraft Company, 527 Fifth
Avenue, New York City, I telephoned Mr. Gaudine and in-
894.24/899
quired whether or not there was any truth in this report.
Mr. Gaudine vigorously denied that the report had any
foundation, and said that the person responsible for the
report was "a liar". Mr. Gaudine denied that he owned
any molybdenum, that he was attempting to sell or export
any molybdenum to any country, and that he was acting
for or on behalf of any other person or firm in con-
APR
nection with the sale of molybdenum. It finally appeared
51940
in the course of our conversation, however, that
Mr. Gaudine had recently made a personal inquiry in regard
to molybdenum at the office of the Climax Molybdenum Cor-
G.
poration and that he had asked an officlal of the New
A
York customhouse in regard to restrictions on the ex-
portation of molybdenum. Mr. Gaudine insiste&, however,
that

=== Page 9 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
that these inquiries had been made purely from "curiosity".
He assured me that he intended to cooperate fully with
the Government in the enforcement of the moral embargo.
He said that he expected to be in Washington tomorrow
or the next day and that he would stop in and see me.
It would appear probable that Mr. Gaudine may have
been inquiring on behalf of some foreign interest in
regard to molybdenum available for export but that he
may have been frightened off by the official reaction to
his inquiries.
wy
Charles W. Yost
I telephoned Mr. Harris this afternoon and told him
of Mr. Yost's conversation with Mr. Gaudine.
J. C. G.

1—1608
Mr. Viles telephoned on April 20 to say that 1t was
reported in the trade that, in addition to 85 tons of
Brazilian rubber imported from Brazil during the early
part of the month, for reexportation to Japan on the S.s.
KANSAIMARU on April 16, 1t was understood in the trade
that another shipment of 180 tons (550 packages) of
Brazilian rubber from Manaos had just been landed in New
R
FILED
York from a Moore-McCormack ship. It was understood that
G
1940
this shipment was a part of a 600 ton lot, all of which
was to be reexported to Japan on Japanese vessels, con-
signed to the Amtorg Corporation.
Mr. Viles telephoned again this morning to pass on
the information which he had seoured but had not fully
cheoked

=== Page 11 === (confidence 0.97)
checked, that the following American concerns had taken
part in the deals above mentioned, either as dealers
or brokers for the Amtorg Corporation: the Frank B. Ross
Company, 507 - Eighth Street, Hoboken, New Jersey, and
Steinharter and Nordlinger, 99 Hudson Street, New York
City. Mr. Viles said that the Frank B. Ross Company
belongs to the Rubber Trade Association and is fully
informed of the Government's nolicy, and he suggested
therefore that the Department call the report to the
attention of that Association so that, at the request of
the Department, they mi ht check up on it more closely
than would otherwise be nossible; & direct letter to the
Company from the Department of State might be used later
1f necessary to follow up. He said that Steinharter and
Nordlinger do not belong to the Association and that the
Department might wish to call the Government's policy
directly to their attention.

=== Page 57 === (confidence 0.95)
PETITION TO BE SUBMITTED TO THE PRESIDENT OF THE
UNITED STATES AND CONGRESS ON THE JAPANESE INVASION OF CHINA.
WHEREAS, the people of the United States desire peace and deplore
international lawlessness and aggression, and
WHEREAS, the Japanese invasion of China, fomented by the military party of
Japan, has led the United States of America and many other nations to indict
Japan as a violator of the Nine-Power Treaty and the Kellogg pact, and to
protest repeatedly against the mcrciless bombing of undefended Chinese cities,
the unnecessiry slaughter of Chinese civilians, and the destruction and
imperilment of schools, churches, hospitals, and business enterprises in
which their nationals are engaged, and
WHEREAS, we feel put to shame that American raw materials are being used
to manufacture weapons of war in Japan, that American planes are being used
to carry bombs from America for the destruction of Chinese civilian
populations, that shells from America are loading Japanese guns, and that
America is supplying to Japan huge quantities of scrap iron, oil, trucks,
and other matcriils us d in the furtherance of the war, over and above the
arount of our normal trad. to rucent years, and
WHEREAS, we sympathize with the Chinesc people in their desire to build up
an independent, democratice stite, n end gravely threatened by the present
aggrossion of Japan, and deplore the traffic which makes it possible for
certain American interests to profit from th wanton dostruction of the
innocent victims of Japan se aggression,
THEREFORE, we the undersigned respectfully petition the President of the
United States and Congr ss, in accordance with the principle of the Good
Neighbor and in the interests of the people of the United States, China and
Japan, to take every practicable means, diruct or indirect, to bring to an
cnd a traffic from our country which is compelling us to be a partner in
the destmuction of the Chinese people.
signcalled /r 0. Juf te
1635 Madisonst ur
Chucch
Vice-Chainman.
Intormational Relatins Dept.
WaahnigtmD..

PETITION TO BE SUBMITTED TO THE PRESIDENT OF THE

WHEREAS, the people of the United States desire peace and deplore
international lawlessness and aggression, and
WHEREAS, the Japanese invasion of China, fomented by the military party of
Japan, has led the United States of America and many other nations to indict
Japan as a violator of the Nine-Power Treaty and the Kellogg pact, and to
protest repeatedly against the merciless bombing of undefended Chinese cities,
the unnecessary slaughter of Chinese civilians, and the destruction and
imperilment of schools, churches, hospitals, and business enterprises in
which their nationals are engaged, and
WHEREAS, we fccl put to shame that American raw matcrials are being used
to manufacture weapons of war in Japan, that American planes are being used
to carry bombs from Americe for the destruction of Chinese civilian
populations, that shells from America are loading Japanese guns, and that
America is supplying to Japan huge quantities of scrap iron, oil, trucks,
and other materials used in the furtherance of the war, over and above the
arount of our normal tradc to rcent years, and
WHEREAS, we sympathize with the Chincsc people in their desire to build up
an independent, democratice stitc, n end gravely threatened by the present
aggression of Japan, and deplore the traffic which makes it possible for
certain American interests to profit from the wanton destruction of the
innocent victims of Japanese aggression,
THEREFORE, we the undersigned respectfully petition the President of the
Unitcd States and Congrc ss, in accordance with the principle of the Good
Neighbor and in the intcrests of the people of the United Siates, China and
Japan, to take every practicable means, direct or indirect, to bring to an
end a traffic from our country which is compelling us to be a partner in
the destruction of the Chinese people.

Japan, has led the United States of America and many other nations to indict
Japan as a violator of the Nine-Power Treaty and the Kellogg pact, and to
protest repeatedly against the merciless bombing of undefended Chinese cities,
the unnecessary slaughter of Chinesc civilians, and the destruction and
imperilment of schools, churches, hospitals, and business enterprises in
which their nationals are engaged, and
WHEREAS, wc fccl put to shame that American raw matcrials arc bcing used
to manufacture weapons of war in Japan, that American planes are being used
to carry bombs from Americe for the destruction of Chinese civilian
populations, that shells from America are loading Japanese guns, and that
America is supplying to Japan huge quantitics of scrap iron, oil, trucks,
and other materials used in the furtherance of the war, over and above the
aount of our normal trade to rucent ycars, and
WHEREAS, we sympathize with the Chinesc people in thcir desire to build up
an ind-pendent, democratice statc, n end gravely thrcatened by the present
aggrossion of Japan, and deplore the traffic which makes it possible for
certain American interests to profit from the wanton dostruction of the
innocent victims of Japanese aggression,
THEREFORE, we the undersigned respectfully petition the President of the
Unitcd States and Congross, in accordance with the principle of the Good
Ncighbor and in the interests of the people of the United States, China and
Japan, to take every practicable means, direct or indirect, to bring to an
end &. traffic from our country which is compelling us to be a partner in
the destruction of the Chinese pcople.

populatiors, that shells from America are loading Japanese guns, and that
Aerica is suppl ring to Japan huge quantitics of scrap iron, oil, trucks,
and other nnterials us d in th furtherance of the var, over and above the
crount of our normal trade to r cent y ars, and
WHEREAs, we sympathize with the Chin se people in their desire to build up
in independent, denocratice state, n end gravely threatened by the present
aggression of Japan, and deplor: th traffic which makes it possible for
certain American interosts to profit fron th wanton destruction of the
innocent victims of Japan se aggression,
THEREFORE, we the undersigned respectfully petition the President of the
Unitcd States and Congr ss, in accordance with the principle of the Good
N ighbor and in the interests of the people of the United States, China and
Japan, to take every practicable means, direct or indirect, to bring to an
end a traffic from our country which is compelling us to be a partner in
the destruction of the Chinese people.

WHEREAS, the people of the United States desire peace and deplore
international lawlessness and aggression, and
WHEREAS, the Japanese invasion of China, fomented by the military party of
Japan, has led the United States of America and many other nations to indict
Japan as a violator of thc Nine-Power Treaty and the Kellogg pact, and to
protest repeatedly against the morciless bombing of undefended Chinese cities,
the unnecessiry slaughter of Chinese civilians, and the destruction and
imperilment of schools, churches, hospitals, and business enterprises in
which their nationals are cngaged, and
WHEREAS, we feel put to shame that American raw materials are bcing used
to manufacture weapons of war in Japan, that American planes are being used
to carry bombs from Americe for the destruction of Chinese civilian
populations, that shells from America are loading Japanese guns, and that
America is supplying to Japan huge quantities of scrap iron, oil, trucks,
and other naterials us d in th furtherance of the var, over and above the
amount of our normal trade to r cent years, and
WHEREAS, we sympathize with the Chinesc people in thcir desire to build up
an independent, democratice stitc, n end gravely threatened by the present
aggrossion of Japan, and deplore the traffic which makes it possible for
certain American interests to profit fron the wanton de struction of the
innocent victims of Japanese aggression,
THEREFORE, we the undersigned respectfully petition the President of the
Unitcd States and Congr ss, in accordance with the principle of the Good
Ncighbor and in the interests of the people of the United States, China and
Japan, to take every practicable means, direct or indirect, to bring to an
end a traffic from our country which is compelling us to be a partner in
the destruction of the Chinese pecople.
signed 71LrvP,Cuiloug
211TucleerrnanSt.2.W.

=== Page 147 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF CURRENT INFORMATION
Apr11 25, 1940
Q. Mr. Secretary, there was a story in one of the papers
this morning to the effeot that Japan had protested against
the extension of the so-called moral embargo to include the
equipment and plans for the manufacture of aviation gasoline.
Can you say if that is correct?
A. I haven't checked on it in the 1 st day - such a matter.
As you know we do restrict exports of a number of commodities
for the purpose of conservation for war purpomes. We also
jave a moral embargo on the other hand that relates to the
restrictions on exports of certain commodities by reason of
the use of thep in bombing civilian populations from the air,
contrary to all nations. If there is anything further that I
find out I will be glad to keep it in mind as we go along.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF CONTROLS
MEMORANDUM
Apr11 23, 1940.
894.3472
Co - Mr. Green:
894.24/906
子：
The attached tables of 1mports of petroleum products
1nto Japan and Manchuria during 1937-1939 were sent to
me by P. L. Hopper or the Metals and Minerals Division,
Department of Commerce. These figures were obtained from
the Standard-Vacuum O1l Company, and were made up by
their employees in the field. These men were in a posi-
tion to observe or learn about most petroleum imports
into Japan, and their figures would seem to be fairly
reliable.
This information would appear to be quite val uable
as data of this nature has not been released by the
COLID
Japanese Government. Mr. Hopper said that he was going
to turn this information over to the Office of Naval
Intelligence and the Military Intelligence Division.

to certain countries of airplanes and of certain
materials important in the manufacture of airplanes.
This policy of the American Government rests on
894.24
broad grounds which appear to have the warm support
of the American people.
Specifically included in the moral embargo
1s molybdenum, and the American Government has
asked all American interests to abstain from the

it has imposed the moral embargo. Expert advice

for molybdenum in aircraft manufacture.
三

March 16th, 1940.
NOTES RESPECTING EXPORTS OF NON-FERROUS METALS
FROM CANADA.
Attached hereto is a copy of an article from
the NEW YORK WORLD-TELEGRAH of February 28 th, 1940.
Attention is particularly irected to the
statement on page 2 of the enclosure that,
"The Canaian nickel interests, which control
Canada's output and that of the only other important
source, the French Island possession of New Caledonia
in the South Seas, has not yet curtailed its ship-
ments to Russia or to Japan. ione but the most
innocent doubts that what Germany needs now she can
obtain fron Soviet ussia, her new ally."
This statement is incorrect in three respects:-
(1)
Canada has not exported and the International
Nickel company has not sold any nickel to the
U.S.S.R. s1nce May, 1939. An enquiry for 8,000
tons of nickel, made throuh the Amtorg Corporation
of New York, in October, 1939, was refused by the
Company after consulting the United K ngdom and
Canadian Governnents."In response to subsequent
enquiries for smaller quantities of nickel, the
Canadian and United Kingdom Governments agreed, in
principle, before the Soviet invasion of Finland,
to make available 2,000 tons of nickel to the
U.S.S.R. at the rate of not more than 5o0 tons a
month from the nickel stocks held by the Mond
Nickel Company in the United Kingdom, subject to
satisfactory assurances against re-export to enemy
countries. The Soviet authorities objected to
these conditions and stipulated that they should
be allowed to take delivery of the nickel ex United
States ports. In these circumstances, the offer
of accommodation was withdrawn so that no nickel
has been sold, either by the International Nickel
Company or by the Falconbridge Nickel Company, the
only Canadian producers, to the U.S.S.R., since a
date some months prior to the outbreak of the war.
(2)
Since September 20th, 1939, the export of
nickel from Canada to all destinations has been
subject to export permit control. From that date
to this, export permits have only been granted for
nickel for Japan in respect of quantities covered
by firm-pre-war contracts between the International
Niokel Company and the Mitsui Company. These out-
standing contracts provided for the delivery of
2,435 tons of nickel, of which 1,600 tons were allowed
to go forward in 400-ton shipments in the last quarter
of 1939 and the remaining 835 tons were shipped in two
Instalments, in January and February of this year.
In November and December, 1939, the Mitsu1 Company
endeavoured, wnsuccessfully, to place additional
ordera/

=== Page 167 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
orders for 3,O00 tons of nickel with the International
Nickel Company.
Advice has been received that Japan
wishes to buy 10,O00 metric tons of nickel during the
current year, over and above the 3,Oo0 tons which they
tried to buy in the autumn of 1939. In recent weeks
the Japanese have been urgently requesting that per-
mission be granted for the immediate export of 800
tons of nickel to Japan, and failing that, of at least
200 tons.
. The Canadian authorities have refused to
issue export permits for either quantity and have
given no commitment in respect of future export policy.
(3)
As a matter of record, the International Nickel
Company does not control the nickel mines in New
Caledonia.
Attached hereto is a confidential statement
from the Dominion ureau of Statistics, sl:owin exports to
Japan of copper, nickel, aluminun, lead and zinc since the
outbreak of the war.
The statistics for the month of
September, 1939, are sl:own separately, as the system of
export control was not introluced until Septeber 20th,
1939, and was not, for practical purposes, really operative
until the first week of October. Export compilations, it
may be added, uo not cover a true calendar month, for the
ledgers are closed on the last workin day of each month
and include all custons entries received in Ottawa on that
date. For example, Customs entries passed at Vancouver,
Halifax or St. John on the last days of each calendar month
would be included in the trade fi ures for the following
month. For this reason, it is rarely possible to establish
an exact correlation between trade statistics and export
permits.
The following notes on the various items includ-
ed in this tabular statement of exports of non-ferrous metals
to Japan may be of interest.
COPPER
The export of copper concentrates to Japan
represents the production of a single firm, the Granby
Consolidated Mining and Smelting Company, which ships
the total output of its mine at Copper Mountain,
British Columbia, to Japan in concentrate form, under
a long-term (three year) contract. This firm, and
the Howe Sound Mining and Smelting Company, both of
whioh, inoidentally, are entirely United States owned,
are the only important copper produoers in British
Columbia/

=== Page 168 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
Columbia. They are, too, the only copper producers
in Canada which refused to participate in the arrange-
ments for the bulk purchase of the Janadian copper
output by the United Kingdom Ministry of Supply. They
pled their difficulties as high-cost producers, the
absence of adequate refinin facilities on the West
Coast and their dependence on the Japanese market, to
which they are bound by long-term contracts, as con-
siderations justifyin their refusal to participate in
supplying United Kingdom requirements. Incidentally,
it is understood that a substantial part of the lowe
Sound Company's copper production, all of which is sold
in ore to the American Mining and Smelting Company's
refinery at Tacoma, is ultimately re-exported in
refined form to Japan and to tl.e U.S.S.R. from the
United States.
The item "copper bars" in the tabular statement
refers to certain shlpments of copper cathode plates
made 1n ecember, 1939, and January, 1940. Th1s 1s
not believed to be a normal form of Janadian copper
export to Japan, and enquiries are bein made into
the granting of export permits for thie shipments shown
in the table.
NICKEL
There is little to add to the explanation iven
earlier in these notes re ardin the position in
respect of nickel exports to Japan, except to point
out that the discrepancy between the export return
showing 3,0o0 tons of nickel exported to Japan in the
five months be innini October 1st, 1939, and the
quantity for which export permits were granted, viz.,
2,435 tons, is due to the fact that exports in the
last half of September from Pacific Coast ports are
included in the October total in the regular trade
returns.
ALUMINUM
Exports of aluminum to Japan since the institu-
tion of the system of export permit control have been
reduced to 4,750 tons against 14,000 tons in the
corresponding period last year. No export permits
have thus far been granted, except in fulfilment of
firm pre-war commitments made by the Aluminum Company
of Canada to its Japanese customers. There are at
present 8,200 tons of aluminum outstanding on these
unfilled orders. No export permits have been granted
for aluminum for the past six weeks and the Japanese
have been given to understand that permission to
export any part of the balance outstanding on these
contracts will be governed by the exigencies of the
supply position in Canada and in the United Kingdom.
Under the terms of the Aluminum Company's agreement
with the United Kingdom Ministry of Supply, the
latter has contracted to take the whole Canadian
exportable surplus of aluminum once pre-war commit-
ments (to the United States and Japan) have been
liquidated.
LEAD AND ZINC
Exports of lead to Japan in the last five months
have inoreased to 19,000 tons as against 15,000 tons
in the corresponding period a year ago, while exports
of sino have deoreased from 10,000 tons to 400 tons
over the same period. The only observation to be
made at the moment about the trade in these metals is
that/

=== Page 169 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
that, in the case of lead, the United kingdom Ministry
of Supply contraeted for tie delivery of a fixed quan-
tity which left a substantial balance in the hands of
the producers (the Consolidated Mining and Smelting
Company of Trail), to be disposed of in what markets
they could find, whllo in the case of zinc, the
Ministry of Supply boucht the whole Canadian output
less domestic requirements.
Hence the variations in
the export trade in these metals to Japan in recent
months.

=== Page 170 === (confidence 0.99)
NE YORK WORLL-TELEGRAH
February 28, 1940.
U. S. METAL TRADES SQUEEZED BY ALLIES
Contravene Neutrality Act with Capital's Aid
By the Associated Press.
London, Feb.28.- The FINAnOIAL TIMES today reported
that the New York Commodity Exchance had forbidden its
members to buy tin for re-export.
BY ALLAN KELLER
World-Tele ram Staff writer.
Wit) the aid of the Roosevelt Administration, Great
Britain and France have en ineered a masterful squeeze play at
the expense of this country's producers of certain metal
alloys, which not only contravenes the Heutrality Act but also
injures our chances of buildin up a trade in these materials
after the end of the war.
That statement epitomizes t! e compleints of several
of America's outstandin procucers, trade experts and shipping
men who have made supportin information available to the
World-Telegram.
laving induce te Presicent tn t'e State Depart-
ment to apply the moral ebar o a ainst sales of such valuable
metals as copper ana molybdenun to nations that borb civilian
populations and non-military objectives, the Allied powers
Immediately stepped up tl eir shipments of substitute ores to
the very "inhuman" nations wh ich had hitherto been our best
markets.
FO GUN BARRELS
Molybdenum is a metal of great strategic value.
Added to high-test steel it produces an alloy of great tensile
strength and one that does not "creep", a term that covers the
expansion and molecular alteration that comes with the applica-
tion of great heat. Because of its lightness, it is doubly
1mportant and is used extensively in the manufacture of the
best gun barrels and rifles, and in the fabrication of power-
ful but light airplanes.
The United States enjoys a near monopoly on the
mining of molybdenum, with three major producers controlling
about 90 per cent of the world's output. On Dec. 2 President
Roosevelt established the so-called moral embargo, pointing
out that the American government and the American people
"have for some time pursued a policy of whole-heartedly con-
demning the unprovoked bombing and machine-gunning of civilian
populations from the air".
NO SALES CURB
"This government hopes", Mr. Roosevelt said, "to the
end that such unprovoked bombing shall not be given material
encouragement in the light of recent recurrence of such acts
(Soviet bombing of Finnish cities) that American manufacturers
and exporters of airplanes, aeronautical equipment and
materials essential to airplane manufacture, will bear this
fact in mind before negotiating contracts for the exportation
of these articles to the nations obviously guilty of such
unprovoked bombing."
A short/

=== Page 171 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
A short time later Joseph C. Green, head of the
Ivision of Munitions Control of the State vepartment, wrote
to the molybdenun and aluminum producers and specifically
directed their attention to the President's appeal. This,
naturally, had the effect of a law, without any legislation
having been enacted. Companies that miht have defied the
moral embargo would have been called to account, their books
studied and their time taken up with long investigations and
red tape. So, motivated both by fear of overnment inter-
ference and a high moral resolve to hamstring the brutal-
itarian powers, the American companies stopped all sales.
That was three months ago, roughly, and today
military experts and metallurgists agree that the embargo
is a joke, that it has not disconcerted the munitions makers
of Germany, lussia and Japan, and that it has played directly
into the hands of Great Britain and France, producers of
virtually all of the worlu's supply of nickel, one of the
two interchan eable substitutes for molybdenum.
The Canadian Hickel interests, which control
Canada's output an that of the only other important source,
the French island possession of Rew Caledonia in the South
Seas, has not yet curtaileu its shipments to tussia or to
Japan. None but the most innocent doubts thet what Germany
needs now she can obtain from Soviet Russia, her new ally.
There is one other notal useful for the protuction
of hi h-rade steel. This 1s tun sten, wlicl Jhina controls
with a few minor exceptions. To ain international exchange
for wagin: her wer with Japan, china has shipped 50 per cent
of her tungsten to Russia. Wile the processes involving
the use of nickel and tumgsten are sliltly nore involved
than that utilizing molybdenum, the finished product is just
as rood, and the embargo to which American manufacturers and
miners are adhering is tlus a tragic gesture injurin no one
but our own citizens.
The squeeze play has cone further. Because we
have voluntarlly lost our best markets for this metal the
British and French, who woula normally take much of it for
their own purposes, have been able to beat down the price
by use of the competing substances, nickel and tungsten.
The theory behind the embargo was logical. America
produces all the molybdenum of importance in the world except
for small deposits in Morocco and Norway. Norway needs her
own supply for her munitions works and ships any surplus only
to Sweden. France naturally controls the Moroccan output.
It looked like an open and shut case. The jokers in the deal
turned up, first, when it was learned that Russia and Japan
had anticipated a possible embargo and had laid in about four
years' supply, and, second, when the Allied powers, which had
appealed to this country to withhold the sinews of war, went
right on shipping substitute ores to the nations we had
crossed off our list.
Some obaervers believe that the embargo temporarily
worried Japan and that, as a result, the military clique in
that country pressed for tangible concessions to this nation
by opening wider the Yangtse River for trade by neutrals and
by seeking to consolidate their gains in China with a view to
stepping out. Later the scare wore off and the army recovered
1ts aplomb. They had rechecked their reserves and found them
ample.
In the/

=== Page 172 === (confidence 0.98)
-3
In tle first ten months of last year the Soviet
Un1on took 11,032, 000 pounds, or 41 per cent, and Japan
7,830,000 pounds, or 1 per cent of this country's output of
the metal. Exports to both of these nations jumped shaply
in September and October after war started in Europe. Be-
cause only one-half of one per cent of the metal in a gun
barrel is molybdenum - if that alloy is used - those quantities
that beat the embar o formed a sizable reserve.
The Climax Molybdenum Co., which mines the molyb-
denum in Colorado is the larcest prohucer in this country.
The Kennecott Copper Co., recovers sone of the metal as a
by-product in its mines near Salt Lake City. The Anaconda
Copper o., throu a subsidiary, Jananea of Hexico, mines
a quantity of the ore in Sonora. All of these companies
have obeyed President Roosevelt's appeal, at reat loss to
their stockholders.
Some observers see a potentially dancerous situa-
tion in the fact tiat Anaconda's properties are in Mexico.
If Mexico's covernment, rown eady on t! e wine of expropria-
tion of our  oil properties, were to tale over the mines
producing molybdenun s!e would find a ready market for the
ore in Kussia and Japan. Russia is already making Hanzanillo
one of its chief ports of call for materials essential to the
wagin of war.
American manufacturers an: mine owners, frankly
perplexed at the effeets of the moral enbar o, which runs
counter to the heutralit Aet whiel. permits sales to any
country ready to pay casi on ti e barrel heu! for our oods
and wlich can take then away in its own boats - both fussia
and Japan are in this class - are now awaiting with new
concern the arrival of another Allied mission to this country.
The mission will seek to cet the administration
to add new materials to the moral embargo list and the
American producers are wondering where the ax will fall next.

=== Page 175 === (confidence 0.99)
RIPON KNITTING WORKS
RIPON, WISCONSIN
Apr1l 24, 1940.
Honorable Alexander Wiley,
Senate Office Builäing,
Washington, D. C.
Dear Senator Wiley:-
With further reference to what I wrote you some time
ago regarding the shipment of scrap iron, gasoline, eto.,
to Japan, I noted with interest your speech made last
January on this subject.
In spite of what you said at that time, I feel very
strongly that we are making a serious and suicidal mistake
in not taking prompt and drastic action to stop this support
of a nation which is not only an aggressor against a
friendly power but is a flagrant treaty breaker with regard
to our own nation.
I can assure you that the great majority of people of
this locality feel that Japan is with our own aid building
up a power in the Far East which cannot but be a serious
menace to our owm nation - not only politically but
economioally - and that we are helping her to do this by
supplying her with the goods she needs to carry out her
aims and which she cannot obtain from any other source.
My feeling 1s that Congress should promptly - and
without hesitation - pass legislation prohibiting trade
relations of any kind with a country whioh has broken its
treaty with us. While at the present time this would
probably affect only Japan, it would serve as a warning to
other powers that we cannot and will not tolerate thie sort
of treatment. I believe that even a threat of such legis-
lation would act as a ourb on Japan's further enoroachments
on America's vital interests in the Pacifio.
In writing as I do I speak not only for myself but for
a large number of my friends and acquaintances who are
reluotant to write their Representatives in Congress but
still feel very strongly on this subjeot.
Yours very truly,
W. E. HASELTINE.
WEH/C

WHEREAS 8O0 people have gathered in this auditorium tonight to
demonstrate their desire for world peace
WHEREAS the United States government by permitting shipments of
war materials to Japan, has openly encouraged Japanese violation
of world peace in her eggression in China
WHEREAS the American people have repeatedly voiced their over-
whelming support for China and their disapproval of these govern-
montal policies. And the Congress in spite of this has refused
to tske action for nearly three years
THEREFORE be it resolved: that this meeting go on record as de-
manding that the Congress immediately enact legislation to stop
the shipment of war materials to Japan.

APR 9 -1940
THE "MORAL EMBARGO"
Members of the American aircraft industry are firmly in accord with
the President's condemnation of those foreign countries guilty of "unprovoked
bombing of civilian populations." Unlike most citizens and industries which
have endorsed this stand at no material cost to themselves the aircraft and
allied manufacturers, together with their workers, have paid dearly as the
894.24/91
result of the wholly ineffective expression of this policy. It is a matter
of record in the Department of State that there have been no flagrant vio-
lations of either the letter or spirit of the "Moral Embargo" enunciated
in 1938 as affecting only specified arms, munitions and implements of war
5
and the broadened interpretation of December 1939 which, with its March 1940
indefinite extension, admonishes against the direct or indirect shipment
to proscribed countrics of "any aircraft, aircraft armament, aircraft engines,
aircraft parts, aircraft accessories..........acronautical equipment of all
kinds and to materials essential to aircraft manufacture" whether or not
an export license is required under Soction 12 (i) of the Neutrality Act of
1939.
The American aircraft industry has boen singled out in this "tokon"
embargo. The moral of tho policy is neither questioned nor discussed here
MAY
since the full objective, i.e., cessation of unprovoked bombing of civilian
FILND
population is praisewerthy. We will, however, adduce hereafter facts to prot
1940
that the measures thus far adopted in application and onforcemont of the
moral embargo" not only are inadequate but are producing reverse results
ding to adversely affect tho netional and economic security of the United

=== Page 201 === (confidence 0.99)
2
While this statement is primarily designed to point out the fallacies
in the application to Japan of the "moral embargo" certain of the general
conclusions apply equally to Russia.
It might be well to consider at the outset which, in the case of the
Japanese aggression in China, is the greatest outrage against the moral
standards of civilized society - the sporadic air raids which indiscriminately
have taken toll of civilians and soldiers, the wholesale violation of Chinese
women, or the insidious Japanese - promoted dope traffic which has presumably
affected both civiliens und soldiers in China. Facing the issue squarely we
are forced to admit that whatever means the Japanose have employed to cxccute
ruthlossly their Chinese program, the remaining indispensable foreign as-
sistance comes from the United States. Cutting off progressively aircraft,
aviation supplies and materials is a case of "straining at gnats and swallow-
ing camels."
As official trade statistics testify, the economic situation in Japan
today and for some time past has not permitted that country to dissipate any
appreciable portion of its dollar exchange on the purchase of luxuries in the
American market. It is a foregone conclusion that practically everything
which Japan buys in this country are, strictly speaking, munitions of war
destined to further Japan's military activities in China. The modern army is
mechanised and Japan is no exception in this case. What American automobiles,
asmembled in American-owned Japanese plants, are convoying troops, trans-
porting ammmition and other military supplies? What scrap metals and
American machine tools are essential to the Japanese manufacture of their
implemonts of war, projectiles, and even aerial bombe? It is axiomatic that

=== Page 202 === (confidence 0.99)
3.
without petroloum highly destructive, modern warfare would be impossible -
troop transport by land or water would be crippled, munitions movements
would be hopelessly slowed down, the military aircraft, whether built in
Japan or imported, would be grounded and thus incapable of bombing civilian
populations.
In a press release dated December 20, 1939 the Department of State
announced that the "moral ombargo" had been extended to stop "delivery to
certain countries of plans, plants, manufacturing rights, or technical
information required for the production of high quality aviation gasoline."
This would be an effective move as directed against a country which had not
previously acquired thc skill of refining so-called "aviation gasoline"
ware it not for the fact that the countries against which this embargo is
injection system that makes possible efficient operation of high speed aviation
engines on low grade fuels. "Aviation gasoline" as far as Japan is concerned
is a misnomer. As long as Japan and Russia can secure ample supplies of the
right grades of crude petroleum their local refineries will produce the fuels
which will keep tho troop and mnition truck trains moving as well as send
the bombing planes on their destructive missions. The primary aid we are
iving to the Japanese army and air force in China is not to be measured by
shipments of aviation equipment or even the limited quantities of what is
declared on export as "aviation gasoline" but rathor by the shipmonts of crude
petroleum of which the United States has been and continues to be the principal
aupplier. Other more limited sources of lower grade petroleum are available to
the Japanese but this is equally truo of aircraft. If the Japs turned to these

=== Page 203 === (confidence 0.99)
4。
other sources for extensive supplies they would probably have to supplement
their barter methods of settlement with dollar exchange derived from our
purchases of silk and other luxury items.
To trace the history of the "moral embargo" on aeronautic shipments
to Japan illustrates what many have described as our "ostrich-with-head-in-
the-sand" policy. Some time prior to July 1, 1938 the Japanese had procured
licenses to manufacture certain types and designs of American aircraft. For
various reasons they had not worked these licenses but continued to give
American manufacturers and labor the benefit of selling them the U. S.-built
planes. Following thc application of the first "moral cmbargo," which affected
only those aeronautical items cnumerated in the President's proclamations
of May 1, 1937, thc Japanese were forced (by our action) to build up their
aircraft industry manufacturing planes (U. S. and other forcign design) from
materials bought extensivcly through American aviation companies. After the
broadened aviation "moral emburgo" came into effect in December 1939 aviation
companies could not longer even collect a brokcrago fee on materials but thore
is ample evidence to support the fact that much of these same materials, which
are not readily distinguishable as destined for aviation use, are being ordered
by the Japs direct from the American producers. Hence, America continues to
feed the fast-growing Japanese aircraft industry. By our own short-sighted
and inconclusive action we have signed away what in normal times has been
and would again be one of the best sustaining export markets for our vital
national defense industry.  Further, we are responsible for creating a strong
competitor whose produots built with low-cost labor may in the years ahead
challonge American aircraft in world markets.
A correlated developmont has beon the recently accelerated Japanese

=== Page 204 === (confidence 0.99)
5
demand for Italian aircraft. Here again our "moral embargo" quirk of foreign
policy has had as another negative accomplishment the upbuilding of a rival
industry in a third country. There is evidence of the fact that in other
quarters (Greece, for example) the Government-controlled Italian aircraft
industry is receiving at least partial payment in dollars for its exports -
Japaneso controllod dollars which cannot bo spent here for planes are quite
probably being transferred to Italian account for the same purpose. The
Italians in turn use these dollar proceeds to buy the American materials
which must be imported by their aircraft industry.
export-import trade with Japan, the United States cannot put an end to nor
appreciably cut down. the bombings of Chinese civilian populations. Rather, by
our eabargo policy thus far we have forced Japanese manufacturing moves and
procurement methads which will perpetuate the very practices we thought to
block. In the interest of realism and fair dealing the American aeronautic
industry asks that one of the following courses of action be immediately taken
with respect to the "moral cmbargo" on trade with Japan and Russia.
1. Lift the moral restraint on aeronautic shipments.
2. Ban, by legislation if necessary, all direct or indirect trade.
3. Sanction trado in aeronautic products which are for commercial ?
aviction use.
On this last point, American manufacturers or others competont to judge
can give setisfactory assurances that cortain types and designs of planes could
not successfully be employed for bombing operations nor could they be converted
abroad for such usc without a major factory operation. The Congress of the
United Statos and the Prosident recognized the validity of this distinction be-
tween aircraft for military and commercial use in Public Law 420 (Export-Import
Bank Bill) passed on February 28, 1940 and signed March 2, 1940.
Apr11 1, 1940.

My dear Admiral:-
The attached confidential letter from our Mr. Van
Karnebeek, transmitted to me by Mr. Walden of the tandard-Vacuum
Oil Company, indicates that the Japanere are endeavoring to get
assurance of increased petroleum supplies from the Dutch East Indies
as a protection in the event t at the Hnited Siates would embargo,
or limit, shipments of such supplies to Japan. As you will see,
it is Mr. Van Karnebeek's impression that they are putting some
pressure on the Dutch Government in this connection.
By way of identification, I wish to point out that
Mr. Van Karnebeek, who writes this letter, is a son of His Excellency,
Jhr., Mr., Dr. H.'A. Van Karnebeek, who is at the present ueen's
Commissioner and Gove rnor of o th Holland. The senior Mr. Van
Karnebeek has been Foreign Minister of Holland, and vi the first
President of the League of Nationa.
He is the Chairman of the Board of our operating
company in the Dutch East Indies, and his son has been for many years
in the employ of our Dutch East Indies operating company, resident
for a number of years in the Dutch East Indies.
It is quite natural that the Government in the
Dutch East Indies would be quite frank with the younger Mr. Van
Karnebeek on account of his Dutch citizenship and relationship
with a very distinguished Dutchman.
I am sending you this in duplicate in case you wish
to forward copy of the letter to another Department.
Yours very truly,
Enclosures.
E. J. Sadler.

N.V. NEDERLANDSCHE KOLONIALE PETROLEUM MAATSCHAPPIJ
Batavia-Centrum, April 1, 1940.
Strictly Confidential
Mr. H. F. Prioleau,
The Hague.
Dear Mr. Prioleau:
I was invited to-day to call on Mr. J. E. van Hoogstraaten,
Head of the Commercial Division of the Department of Economic Affairs, for a
short conferonce, also attended by Mr.Hoekstra of the B.P.M., for the purpose
of tentatively discussing a matter which the Government here in the Indies
considers of paramount importance, also fros an international policical point
of view. We were told that as of late Japanese representatives had reopened
negotiations for certain N.I. products with a view to obtaining larger
imports into Japan. It was intimated confidentially that the Japanese
Minister in The Hague had teken certsin steps at the Ministryaf Foreign
Affairs and that consequently additional pressure could be expected from
the Japanese Government, also in view of the threat of an American embargo
on exports to Japan, which would include oil as one of the most important
items. Mr. van Hoogstraaten anticipated that the B.P.M. and ourselves might
be approsched in the near future by certain Jepanese agents inquiring whether
present shipments to Japen could be increased. He asked us to keep him in-
formed of such requests and also not to turn them down flatly, but rather to
keep matters open so thst further contact would remain possible, which seems
to be the Government's present desire. We were asked to write you about
this matter and to request you to adopt a similar sttitude. I of couree
promised to keep Mr. van Hoogstraaten advised of any advances made by
Japanege representatives in this regard and also to write you as per his
request.
In this connection I should mention that some time ago
Mr. Spit, Vice-President of the Council of India, broached this subject to
me unofficially, at the same time asking me not to take any steps in the
matter or write about it yet since the question was still under discussion
here in the highest Government circles. Mr. Diokover, American Consul-
General at Batavia, aiso seems to have raised this question more than once,
and has tried to find out unofficially whether, in the event of an American
embargo on oil exports to Japan, the N.I. Government would permit increased
exports from here to Japan, thue, of course, to a certain extent nullifying the
American embargo, which is considered of far-reaching sconemic and political
impertance by all concerned. Mr. Spit explained to me that the Government
would of course not wieh to get into trouble with the American Government by
allowing sueh inoreased exports, nor would they wish to offend the Japanese
to any considerable extent. Incidentally, I understand the Japanese have
recently made further attempts through Dutch/Japanese companies to obtain oil
lands in this country and are at a loss to understand why such requests are
still being refused. Mr. Spit emphasised that he had had an opportunity to
discuss this question with the Governor-General, but that it had been decided

=== Page 213 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
tako no further action in the matter for the time being. He also asked me
to advise him what percentage of our production was being exported to Japan,
roughly, and whether we could increase our production and exports if we so desired.
A few days later he asked for this information again, and I told him our exports to
Japan amounted to approximately 8% of a total production of well over 2,l00,000 tons
of crude oil per annum. I did not, however, discuss with Mr. Spit the possibility
of increasing our present production or exports.
Today's conference indicsted, however, that the economic side
of the problem had been handed over to the Department of Economic Affairs for
further development with the companies on an official basis, and Mr. van Hoogstraaten
seems to have been commissioned with this task. I had an opportunity to explain to
him in brief the set-up of our marketing relations with Standard-Vacuum and told
him that no doubt mattere of this kind could best be developed in Holland between
the highest officials and our Principals. Mr. Hoekstra stated that questions of
this nature were taken care of by theil London offices, and in this connection I
might mention that he did not once refer to his Hague office. I assume, however,
that he will write to The Hague on the matter and not to London.
It was the general feeling of those present that in the event of
an American embargo on oil, the entire N.I. production might barely suffice to
meet Japen's requirements, so that a small increase in present exports to Japan
would actually be of little significance. Japan would have to take stronger
measures in order to meet the situation, such as the seizure of one or two oil
porte.
I can assure you that I will do nothing further in the matter
until I hear from you and will observe the utmost caution in any further talks
with the Government or Japanese agents.
In view of the extremely confidential nature of this question,
w have made arrangements with Mr. van Hoogstraaten to forward this letter
through his intermediary via certain channels which the Government has at its
disposal for this purpose, and are consequently sending you the original of this
lotter only, which we trust will not inwonvenience you too much.
Yours very truly,
(Signed) H. A. van Karnebeek

My dear Mr. Green:
727
824.24
You may remember that some time ago I called on you to raisem
one or two questions with regard to contributions made to our Committee,
and left with you a letter covering these points.
8
394.24/9
You may recall that I mentioned to you the matter of our accepting
contributions from American-born members of local Chinese communities.
There seemed to be no question as to the propriety of our receiving a few
such gifts, but because a somewhat larger-than-ordinary contribution has
just come from such a source, I want to mention it to you once again in
order to be perfectly sure that the record, from our standpoint, is straight.
On Friday last a contribution of $1,500 from a Mr. Walter C. James
8
of 66 West Randolph Street, Chicago, was received. This is the second
donation that Mr. James has sent to us. He is an American-born citizen
of Chinese parentage. This contribution is from himself and his friends.
I believe that a majority of the members of the older Chinese communities
in this country, including that in Chicago, are American born and therefore
citizens of this country. With the contribution Mr. James wrote me that he
was suggesting to people in other Chinese-American communities that they al.so
contribute at this time to our Committee's work. I am sending you herewith
a copy of my reply to Mr. James' letter, and I hope that you will have an op-
portunity to read it. If there is any reason at all why we should not accept
his contribution, or gifts from other American-born Chinese in these communiz
ties, I shall appreciate your advising me accordingly.
These gifts would, in any case, represent only a small percentage'
of our Committee's total income, our contributions now being over 7,000 in
number. In no case has there been raised any question of an effort to in-S
fluence or control our movement in the slightest degree. I am simply for-
warding all of this to you so that we may be fully advised if there is the
slightest question of legality involved in our accepting these contributions.
If there is, we should of course return this gift and refuse others.

One further point I should like to mention also. To
Chinese friends who have asked how they might be of help, we have oc-
casionally suggested that they discuss the question with their American
friends and that, in cases where they felt it was appropriate to do so,
they might ask these friends, if they were sufficiently interested, to
cooperate with our Committee in its efforts. One of those who asked us
how he might be of any help was Dr. Lin Yu-tang, eminent Chinese author.
I suggested the possibility of his sending a letter to some of those who
were particularly interested in the Far East, or who might have ordered
and read some of his books. This he has done, and I enclose a copy of
the letter which he has let me have. It seemed that this was a perfectly
legitimate activity on his part and that it would not involve us in any
charge of subterfuge, it being simply a case of a private Chinese citizen
suggesting openly to American friends that this was one way in which they
could be of help to the people of his country, and even to those of Japan
as well, if they wished to do so.
I am also enclosing an extra copy for your files of a
recent report on the progress and program of this Committee. We have
found most encouraging the very widespread and sustained interest and
support that have come to our movement from all parts of the country.
Very sincerely yours,
Hany S Theea

April, 1940.
Dear Mr. Olemonts:
A situation has arisen in which, through nobody's
fault, America finds herself occupying the unenviable po-
sition of being the only nation whose economic alliance with
Japan is vitally necessary to the continuance of the war in
China. Since Japan can never conquer the whole of China,
not even consolidate her present conquests, the continuance
of this war only meane the continuance of suffering both for
the Japanese and the Chinese people.
America today faces a dilemma from which there is no
escape. She must either, commercially, help Japan to fight
China, or she must, by various trade restrictions, help China
to fight Japan. There is no third alternative.
As a Chinese, I cannot do anything to help directly
the American Committee for Non-Participation in Japanese Ag-
gression, which is headed by Hon. Henry L. Stimson. It is,
and always will be, a hundred per cent American organization,
financed and directed by Americans. It is, however, the best
organization working for a cause with which so many Americans
are in sympathy. You can best help this cause by helping this
Committee with your cooperation and with generous funds with
which to carry on and intensify its work. You are helping to
terminate the sufferings of the Chinese and Japanese people
when you help this Committee.
Please pardon this direct appeal. I am sure you will
underetand the compulsion that motivates one whose people are
being subjected to brutal and ruthless aggression.
Yours sincerely.
P.S. Please send your contributlon and 1nquiries to Mrs. Sidney
D. Gamble, Treasurer, The Amerioan Committee For Non-Par-
ticipation in Japanese Aggression, 8 West 40th St., New
York, N.Y.

RECORD OF PROGRESS
Beginnings
The invasion of China started on July 7, 1937. But it was
not until the following spring that trade statistios revealed Japan's
extensive dependence upon the United States for essential war materials.
A growing recognition that America was, in effect, a powerful ally of
Japan for her program of conquest in Asia led to the spontaneous organ-
ization of The American Committee for Non-Participation in Japanese
Aggression. A planned program of work was begun on August 1, 1938.
Objectives
The following paragraphs are taken from a full statement of
the Committee's objeotives published at that time.
"Neither widespread sympathy for the Chinese people
in their courageous struggle for freedom nor protests over
inhuman warfare and the violation of treaties can atone for
the positive support which we are lending to the Japanese
military machine.
"This Committee's reason for existence is to help in
bringing the essential facts to the attention of the American
people, cooperating with other individuals and organizations
that are working to this end, and to investigate ways and
means whereby the stoppage of war credits and essential war
supplies to Japan can be made effective for the remainder of
the present conflict in China.
"The Committee will endeavor to help in crystallizing
publio demand and support for governmental exeoutive action
designed to cheok the flcw of American credits and war mater-
ials to Japan. Study is being made of the forms of effective
exeoutive action possible under existing law. Contact will
be maintained with the governmental departments concerned.
"The Committee will also work toward seouring the enact-
ment of a measure at the next session of Congress prohibiting
the extension of economio aid for war purposes to a oountry
which is in process of violating a solemn treaty of peace to
whioh the United States is a party.
"The central and single aim of the Committee is expressed
in the phrase 'non-partioipation in Japanese aggression.'
Doctrinal isms, domestio issues, and the European situation,
however important, are beyond its scope.

=== Page 225 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
"Members of the Committee regard this program as a
constructive effort in the direotion of international peace
and justice. They desire the well-being of the people of
Japan as well as of all other peoples in the Paoific area.
They desire peace and justice for all concerned. They feel,
at the same time, that before any just settlement and lasting
peace struoture can be achieved, Japanese military aggression
must be cheoked. They believe that for this a policy of non-
cooperation by the powers together with continued Chinese
resistanoe will be effective, and that the urgent and crucial
need of the moment is to withhold economic assistance for war
purposes from Japan."
Study of the Problem
With the assistance of outstanding authorities, a careful
study was made of the practical questions involved in the placing of
restraint upon American war trade with Japan. A summary discussion of
prinoipal facts and issues was presented in the Committee's first pub-
lication, "America's Share in Japan's War Guilt," which received wide-
spread comment. Continued counsel with experts on the Far East and on
American international relations has remained an indisnensable part of
the Committee's program.
Enliatment of Workers
It was early realized that for an effective, nation-wide
movement to develop, it would be necessary to invite and enlist the
cooperation of other organizations and of thousands of interested,
informed individuals in all parts of the country. Seventy organiza-
tions and publications were individually approached for carefully
selected names and addresses; the response was heartening. This
furnished a "master file" of 22,000 individuals to whom were sent
copies of "America's Share in Japan's War Guilt." From answers to
this mailing, a beginning was made in the building of a seleoted corps
of aotive "friends and supporters." The process begun in this way
has beem continuous ever since. New mailings to new groups have fol-
lowed eaoh other in steady sucoession. The Committee now has a master
file of nearly 100,000 key names, over 12,000 of whom have shown an
intereet suffioient to enable us to place them in our active file of
friende and supporters. Probably the greatest single asset of the
Committee up to the present time has been this informed and coordinated
body of frionds in every part of the nation, among whom are many people
of outstanding influence, national or in their own commurities.

=== Page 226 === (confidence 0.97)
一一
The Committee has felt it ex eedingly important to maintain
a living relntionship with this constantly growing list of friends and
supporters. A succession of thirty mailings, each containing fresh
information, literature, and suggestions for action, have been sent to
the members of this group, many of whom have be come, in turn, centers
of information and influence in their own communities. It has been
possible in this way to realize substantial and focussed expressions of
oublic ooinicn at just the times when these would accomolish the most.
The volune of individual personal correspondence that has grown out of
these efferts has been rether overwhelming. orm renlies have been
increasingly used. But the Committee has felt the importance of keeping
relationships personal and not stereotyped, and from 200 to 300 carefully
written personal letters are now sent out weekly.
Leadership
As the Committee har eatablished itcelf and demonstrated
soundness and responsibility in its werk, i' hns been able to secure,
increasingly, nationally respeeted leaders ip. Jerving with Ar. Henry
L. Stimson, honorary chairman of the Committee, are the following
honorary vice-chairmen: r. Henry I. Harriman, former president of
the U. S. Shamber of Commeree; :r. A. Lawrence Loweli, president
emeritus of larvard University: Ir. Robert E. speer, former president
of the Federal Council of Churches: ir. illiam Alier. " ite, dean among
American newspaner publishers: and hear Admiral i. E. Yarnell (retired),
former commar.der-in-chief of the U. 3. Asiatic Fleet. These and many
other nationelly krown and respected citizens have come to the active

lending of their names, they have contributed gr atly to the develop-

Cooo rating Local 'ommittees
About eighty local committees cooperating actively with
The American Committee have developed. Amorg particularly strong
committees are those in Ames (Iowe), Boston, Baltimore. Washington,
Richmond, Durhan (N.C.), Pittsburgh, Cleveland, Toledo, Columbus (Ohio),

Pasadena, Portland, San Francisco, and Los Angeles. The continuous
interohange of information and suggestions between cooperating committees
and the national organization have been exceedingly heloful. These com-
mittees have been especially active in el.listing local leadership,
setting up meetings, distributing literature, securing newspaper and
radio publicity, and extending greatly the volume of genuine popular
demand for restrictions upon our decisive help to Japan.

Other Cooperating Organizations
The Amerioan Committee has endeavored to make itself as useful
as possible to other national and local organizations interested in
America's international relations and, when practicable, to use existing
ohannels for the distribution of information and the enlistment of posi-
tive public support. These organizations have included church groups,
business associations, civic and professional olubs, labor unions,
women's organizations, foreign policy study groups, and the like. To
date, national and local organizations representing a total membership
of well over 40,000,C00 people have formally protested against American
aid to Japan (or, in some cases, to aggressors in general) for war pur-
poses. The effort has been made to cooperate with each organization in
a way consistent with its particular background, basis of interest, and
organizational structure.

the preparation and wide distribution of informative and arresting liter-

Mailings to Special Groups
Special mailings have helped to extend discussion through
the use of material adapted to the interests of particular groups.
The largest such mailing was o the l02,000 Protestant ministers of
America. Others have been sent to: chief editorial writers of lead-
ing newspapers, prominent cotton growers and brokers, steel producers,
members of the Republican Progran Committee, branches of The American
Union for Concerted Peace fforts, editors of Catholic papers, American
missionaries from China, friends of Chinese education, writers and
speakers on the Far East and on international affairs.
Speakers
Lackirg the necessary funds and personnel for setting up a
full-fledged Speakers' Bureau, the Committee has nevertheless been
instrumental in arranging several hundred speaking engagenents for
competent interpreters of the Far Eastern sitistion. Inquiries con-
cerning speakers are now hurdled daily.
Press P:ulicity
The two greatest channels of public infornation on current
problems are, of course, the press and the radio. Trying to avoid the
elements of emotional or distorted propaganda, the Committee has sent
out 48 factuel newspeper releases. Most of tiese have been utilized
either nationally or locally. We are now finding the press increasingly
receptive to releases from the Comaittee. A few special releases have
been sent to trade journals, and to church papers.
Letters have also been sent to editors, enolosing materials
suitahle for editorial comment, and offering to furnish a few categories
of ad itional information upon request. This has resulted in inquiries
from a number of newspapers. Interviews have also been arranged with
editors of leading newspapers in important centers. A luncheon recently
given for publishers end editors of the New York metropolitan area was
attended by approximately 30 guests. Several cooperating committees
are now undertaking to arrange similar luncheons.

Radio Publicity
The Committee and cooperating organizations have been directly
instrimental in arranging for presentation of the subject of America's
Far Eastern policy over ten national network programs, including the
Town Meeting of the Air, the People's Platform, the Chicago Round-Table.
Through factual material and personal interviews, basic information has
been furnished to radio commentators. Between 100 and 150 local broad-
casts have been arranged, Two electrical transcriptions and several brief
scripts have also been made available to many local stations.
Interviews
During a recent conversation, nn influential newspaper publisher
st:essed the impo tance of continuo s and effective aporoach to the
"fountain heads" of influence. Special interviews have been conducted
with leading editors, business men, lawyers, churchmen, and other influ-
ential citizens in importat centers. It is not possible to estimate the
effects of these interviews. one illustration, however, might be given.
Interviews with publishers and editorial writers of five leading New York
newspapers have resulted in the use of materials furnished by the Committoe,
and have contributed to an increasingly strong and clearly focussed editorial
policy in each oase.
Financing
From the outset, the Committee has faced the constant necessity
of meeting its operating expenses through the solicitation of gifts and,
to a much less extent, through the sale of literature and a small net
income from speakers.
A proof of growing popular support of the Committee's position
is found in responses to mail appeals for contributions. Seventeen finan-
cial appeals have been mailed to carefully selected and prepared lists:
the total number of letters sent out has been nearly 250,000. Mioh care
has gone into the preparation of these letters in order that in every oase
they might prove stimulating and informing, whether or not they yielded a
finanoial return. Theso efforts have been supplemented by interviews with
individuals, during which it was possible to explain more fully the work
and needs of the Committee. A number of larger sontributions have resulted
from these interviews, and it appears that an increasing number of substan-
tial gifts will be needed if the Committee's program 1s to go forward in a
vigorous way during the period which may elapse before adequate measures
are taken by our Government to curb America's deoisive war aid to Japan.

There are now 19 full-time end 5 part-time workers.

A further evidence of the growth of an active and articulate
publio opinion has come from Washington itself. Members of the State
Department and of Congress speak now of strong public support for the
policies advocated by this Committee. Typical of such comment was a
remark of Congressman Bruce Barton during e hearing of the Foreign
Affairs Committee. He said that there surely could be no doubt in the
minds of any member of the Committee, in view of the hundreds of letters
they were receiving daily, that the overwhelming sentiment of the American
people was in favor of a stoppage of American help to Japan's program in
China.
Washington Relations
It is believed that the Committee's work -- and the public
demand that it has helped to mobilize -- have contributed directly and
substantially to the measures already taken by our Gevernment:
Adoption and extension of the "moral embargo"
Denunciation of the 19ll commercial treaty
Ambassador Grew's warning to the Japanese people
Development of additional credits to China for
economic rehabilitation.
The Committee will continue to support such measures until the basic
conditions for a just peace and for a new neriod of cooperation and
reconstruction in the Pacific ares are realized.
Friendly relations have been consistently maintained with key
members of the Administration and of Congress. Discussion has taken place
at frequent intervals since the outset of the Committee's activities. The
rapid growth in public opinion and public desire for action has led to
increased consultation with government leaders. Several authorities
recommended by the American Committee were invited to testify during hear-
ings conduoted by the Foreign Relations and Foreign Affairs committees of
Congress. The effort has been made to work in harmony with those carrying
the enormous responsibilities involved in the conduct of our foreign re-
lations at this time, while retaining full independenoe of thought and
action. Heartening assurances have been received with regard to the
reputation which the Committee now has among leaders of both parties and
in the Exeoutive branch of the government.
With the establishment of an office in Washington, under the
supervision of Ar. Roger S. Greene, whieh will facilitate the conduot of
interviews by able and informed friends and supporters of the Committee,
it is expected that our Washington relations will be further strengthened.
A word about the philosophy underlying our Washington relations
may be in order. Broadly, it might be said that three main influences
determine the development of specifio policies in a demooracy. First,
there is the oontinuing and changing heritage of information and mis-
information, sentiment and prejudice whioh exists throughout the nation
on any question of publio concern. Second, there is the constant impact

=== Page 232 === (confidence 0.99)
-9-
of the news, with its effect upon popular attitudes and upon the intensity
of public interest. And third, there is conscious effort on the part of
interested groups to influence both public opinion and the course of
government policy. In a dynamic democracy there is continuous interplay
among these complicated influences.
The last named influence is frequently called "pressure," The
arts of propaganda and of political influence have become sufficiently
advanoed to enable pressure groups to operate with powerful effect in a
democracy. These groups are, broadly speaking, of two types: those which
repres nt private or specisl group interests, seeking to maintain or ad-
vance their own advantages in any situation: ond those which represent a
broader public interest, seeking to realize whet might be termed the
greatest good to the greatest number. The first type is more readily
organized. The second, because it cuts across all grouns, is less readily
mobilized and made artic late and effective. The survival and healthy
finctioning of a democracy may depend upon the ability of those who take
the larger view to think and act realistically, with sufficient clarity,
unity, and strength to make their views effective. The competitive inter-
olay of special interests is not nough. of course, there are honest
differences of opinior among those who take a larger view, and these must
be tested on the anvils of public discussion and debate.
Those working for the otjectives of The American Committee for
Non-Participation in Japanese Ag ression have, for the most part, endeavored
to see the Far Eastern situntion and American Fer Eastern policies as a
whole, and to work for a solution which, in the long run, would be of great-
est benefit to all concerned, including the Jananese people themselves, and
which would be consistent with the requirements of a more just and durable
pea ce.
From this standpoint, the Committee's work has been focussed,
first, toward the spread of sukstantial information and interest, and
second, toward a realistic and effective mohilizntion of public opinion
in support of political action. No stereotyped formulae have been foisted
upon the public. Rather, an attempt has been made to provide basic in-
formation, with interpretation from a viewpoint clearly stated, and to
invite honest, individual expressions of opinion whether or not they
agreed with the position taken by the Committee. The result has been,
we believe, a more thoughtful and convincing presentation of views to
members of Congress and the Administration than would have been achieved
through inflated, high-pressure methods. Mention has already been made
of the extent of publio supnort for the ourtailment of war aid to Japan,
and it is believed that this nation-wide demand has been wholly genuine.

PROGRAM AND NEEDS
Introductory Note
The attempt, in a short period of time, to place a great
issue in international relations effectively before the American
people is no small undertaking. During the past eighteen months a
good deal has been learned, through trial and error, about the most
effective methods of work and the relative importance of various
approaches. The program covered in the following pages provides
for a continuation and extension of work already in hand and for a
number of special projects whioh, it is believed, will definitely
enhance the effeotiveness of the Committee's program as a whole.
It is difficult to estimate with assurance the length of
time that will be required before our government places more sub-
stantial and effective restrictions upon our crucial war trade with
Japan, or the moments when more intensive effort will be required.
Steps taken up to the present time - the renunciation of the trade
treaty with Japan, Ambassador Grew's warning to the Japanese people,
the extension of the moral embargo, the development of a new credit
for China - have all grown out of public demand for effeotive action.
The promptness and effectiveness of further measures whioh the govern-
ment may feel enabled to take will depend in large measure upon the
steady continuance and mounting of publio demand as well as upon
developments in the Far East and in Japanese-American relations.
It is very important that further action be taken promptly.
The aoproach of party conventions and eleotions, the possibilities
of intensified conflict in Europe, economic and politioal strains in
China as well as in Japan all point to the need of considered and
decisive measures without undue delay.
Further plans for the Committee's wcrk are based upon an
estimated twenty-week program of vigorous effort, with the belief
that if this program can be carried through successfully, the desired
results will be essentially achieved. The estimated finanoial re-
quirements of such a program are outlined in the pages that follow.

6th. May 1940.
Hon. Allen T. Treadway,
Washington, D. C.
My dear Mr. Treadway,
I think that I wrote you some weeks ago about the
proposed embargo on war time supplies to Japan. At that
time I hoped that a moral embargo so-called would be
enough to put a stop to the continuance of the supplies
that our merchants were shipping to Japan. But my
hopes have been disappointed. And I am now convinced
that legal stepc should be taken, at least so far as
to empower the Preeident to embargo all such supplies.
I have been much disappointed with the timidity
of Congress to do things promptly to help out the
struggling democracies in their fight against the
totalitarian powers, and I should like to see the Congress
of the United Statee take a bold and positive stand to do
all possible to help in these critical times.
Yours sincerely
J. FRANKLIN CARTER

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
April 23, 1940.
With reference to Mr. Jones' attached
memorandum it may be observed that if exports
of certain essential war materials were placed
on a quota basis, and the quota for each
country established on the basis of average
exports to each country during the ten years
1909-1918, it would probably be possible on
the basis of a cursory examination of the
figures for those years to restrict drastically
exports to Japan of crude oil, gasoline and
copper, and to a slight extent exports of
lubricating oil without curtailing exports to
France, Canada or the United Kingdon. In 1919
there was a large export to Japan of copper.
There was no substantial export of scrap iron
from the United States to any country prior to
1920.
Notwithstanding the foreroing observa-
tions I concur entirely with the views
expressed by Mr. Jones in recard to the
disadvantages likely to attend any attempt to
restrict exports to any particular country or
countries through the mediun of a quota system.
It may also be mentioned that the period
1909-1918 mentioned above would embrace the
yoars of the European War when exports of war
materials to the European allies were at high
levels. The basing of quotas on a period
whioh would include those years wouid therefore
make it especially transparent that we were
aiming at Japan.
m
