FOB AN ENDURING PEACE
Never have Americans looked beyond the seas with deeper concern
than today. For a decade we have witnessed the breakdown of treaties,
the invasion of weaker nations, the ominous increase of armaments.
Now two continents are locked in conflict, and we sec, more than ever
before, the mobilization of whole peoples, with the submergence of their
ordinary life——their activities, thought, freedoms, and aspirations—to the
grim requirements of nations engaged in a life and death struggle.
With this picture has come the disquieting thought that if forces of
lawlessness gain the ascendency elsewhere, there will be little on which
to build a future peace. Indifference on our part would be impossible.
Facing a world of hostile camps, we would impose upon ourselves a
much more staggering burden of armaments which of themselves would
reduce our prosperity and shorten the spaces that now divide us from
the areas of conflict. We too would experience the restraints, and the
losses of freedom, incurred by nations that are girding seriously for
possible combat in the world of today. Is there nothing that we can do
now, while keeping the peace ourselves, to prevent the coming of such
a day?
Americans have never thought more realistically or more honestly
about the problems of peace than now. There is a growing and disturbing
realization that if we had done our part better, heretofore, in the build
ing of peace, the world might not be where it is today. Many persons
are asking whether there is any way, even now, in which America can
exert its peaceful influence more effectively on the side of law and free-
dom and cooperation, and against ruthless military aggression.
In the Pacific, American influence and responsibility are now para
mount. There exists an opportunity, which may not come again, to help
terminate the present stalemate in China, and to cooperate then in the
building of a more equitable and enduring peace in "half the world".
What are the necessary foundations for such a peace? They may be vari-
ously stated; by thoughtful study and negotiation, their details can be
delineated. Four basic elements are believed to be essential.
1. The ending of Japanese aggression. This will become a reality
when American help to Japan is stopped. We may not feel--with good
reason—that it is our responsibility to stop Japan. But we cannot escape
the responsibility that is now ours for arming Japan, and for thus pre-
venting China from resisting more decisively Japan's military aggression.
2. The restoration of an independent China. It has been well said
that China can regain her independence against Japan alone, but that
she may not be able to do so against a Japan backed by the economic
[38]

=== Page 334 === (confidence 0.99)
resources of the United States of America. A free and friendly China,
progressing internally and disposed to cooperate with other peace-loving
nations, is a factor of inestimable importance in the restoration of se
curity. and in the reestablishment of international relations in the Pacific
area.
3. A new and better machinery for peace. This presents difficult
problems, but the alternative is a return to international anarchy. Much
has been learned during the last twenty-five years It is clear that an im-
posed or vengeful peace, unfair to any nation concerned, is imperma
nent: that effective safeguards against future aggression are necessary.
that channels for the equitable settlement of disputes are essential; and
that means must be provided for overdue or urgently needed changes
by peaceful methods. Most of all, there needs to be a restoration of con
fidence, and good will, and friendly economic and cultural intercourse
between the nations concerned. The essential problems of eastern Asia
and the Pacific are not as difficult or involved as those of Europe. The
United States, in a spirit not of exploitation but of cooperation among
equals, has an unexcelled opportunity to extend the Good Neighbor
policy toward other great nations of the Pacific area.
4. The development of economic cooperation. Political peace with
out economic peace is illusory. With the end of war, both China and
Japan will face exceedingly ditficult problems. They must be helped in
making adjustments involved in shifting to  peace economy, and in
initiating broad efforts toward reconstruction. They must be assured of
access to resources needed for their economic life. and to markets essen-
tial to the development of their industries and trade. The United States
(an help through public and privite credits (unlizing. perhaps, some of
our enormous gold stocks for this purpose), through new commercial
treaties with both countries providing for mutually beneficial trade
relations, and through the encouragement of like treaties between China
and Japan, and between both nations and other Pacific powers. By what-
ever specific methods may be deemed best. the United States will have
the opportunity, once the war is ended, to contribute its responsible and
significant share toward the increasing prosperity and well-being of
Asia and the Americas.
But for this it is essential that we take the first step of making it
possible for China to end the war and to bring about the first great
check to aggression in a decade, by ourselves foregoing the sordid
profits from our war trade with Japan. Only then can there emerge the
necessary foundations for a just and lasting peace in the Pacific.
[39]

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

MEMORANDUM
May 4,
1940.
STATE
Mr. Greed
MAY 181940
Upon the receipt from the Bureau
NOTED
and
Domestic Commerce of the attached table of statistics
894.24
in regard to exports to Japan, Russia, and Mexico during
FW
the first three weeks of April of articles covered by
894.24/93
the various moral embargoes, I telephoned Mr. James Young,
Director of the Bureau, referred to our conversation of
March 30 and asked whether the Bureau had been able
to get any satisfaction from the persons or firms
responeible for the export of $200,000 worth of aero-
2
nautical products to Japan in January. Mr. Young expressed
great surprise that we had received no report concern-
ing the outcome of the representations made by the Bureau
and said that he would look into the matter immediately.
I also referred to the exportation to Japan in April of
aircraft parts and accessories valued at 229,650 which
1s listed in the attached table. Mr. Young said that he
had already noticed this item and that he had directed
that it be investigated.
Later in the day Mr. York of the Automotive Aeronautics
Section of the Bureau telephoned me to report on his
investigation of the shipments of aeronautical products
F/N
to

=== Page 355 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
to Japan both in January and in April. He said that
he had approached the important American manufacturer
responsible for the largest shipment made to Japen in
January (that valued at $74,150), but that he had
obtained no satisfaction from this company. After con-
siderable deley it merely informed him that the aero-
nautical products in question were replacement parts
to be used on commercial planes and that since there is
no law forbidding such exportations it saw no reason why
they shoula not be made.
The remaining shipments in January and all of the
shipments in April Mr. York declared to have been made
by shipping agents in New York and Los Angeles acting
on behalf of Japanese companies having offices in tnis
country. In other words it would anpear that Mitsui,
Mitsubishi and similar firms are buying aircraft parts
and accessories, either directly or through intermediaries,
from American manufacturers or dealers and are then them-
selves exporting these parts end accessories to Japan.
Mr. York said that he had taken no action in regard to
these exportations since he felt that it would be useless
to approach Japanese concerns.
The device, imposed upon us by the Department of
Commerce contention that the law forbids it to reveal
even

=== Page 356 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
oven to the Department of State the names of exporters
signed to export declarations, of endeavoring to enforce
the moral embargo through the agency of the Bureau of
Foreign and Domestic Commerce has not proved to be suc-
cessful. Mr. Young has on every occasion proved to be
most cooperative but I have not had the feeling that the
subordinate officers in the Bureau were endeavoring to
enforce the embargo with any degree of zeal.
They are
naturally more interested in expanding rather than
curtailing foreign trade and I believe that they limit
their efforts to enforce the embargo merely to bringing
its terms to the attention of its violators. Naturally
this has little or no effect. If this phase of the
embargo is to be administered successfully, it would
appear that we must repeat our request to the Department
of Commerce to turn over to us the names of violators.
Though that Department has given the impression thet it
does not have the legal authority to reveal these names,
our preliminary investigation of the pertinent law would
appear to demonstrate that it 1s merely a regulation of
the Department of Commerce rather than the terms of the
lew itself which prevents these names from being revealed
at least to another Government department. We shall
attempt to get a more definite legel opinion on this
point

=== Page 357 === (confidence 0.96)
-4-
oint within the next few days.
The question of how to deal with exportations in
violation of the embargo by Japanese firms in this coun-
try may prove to be even more difficult. It might be
possible that Treasury would be willing to require in
exportations of this sort that the merchandise to be
exported be described fully and that the name of the
American manufacturer be stated on the export declara-
tion. If this were done we could, of couree, aoproach
the manufacturer concerned and request that he not sell
to Japanese interests.
Charles W. Yost

OF STATE

1940.
MAY 14 1940
Department of State
1. Aviation gasoline exports to Japan
declined steadily from January through March
of this year. The total for the quarter
($895,274) was less than one fourth of the
M
total 1939 exports ($5,006,840).
No aviation gasoline was exported to
8
the Soviet Union in the first quarter of
6
1940 and only $1,751 worth in the year 1939:
2. Exports to Japan of regular gasolin
also declined regularly from January through
March of this year. However, the first
quarter total ($970,880) was more than one
6
fourth of the 1939 totai ($2,889,276).
2
Regular gasoline exported to the Soviet
Union in January totaled $186,051. There
were no exports in February and March. The
first quarter total was considerably less
than one quarter of the 1939 total ($1,950,44)
3. Crude petroleum exports to Japan
declined in February as compared with Janu-
ary of this year. Such exports increased in
March but still remained below the January
total. The total for the first quarter
($3,594,129) was less than one quarter of
the total exports for 1939 ($20,924,000)
whioh in turn was less than the 1938 total
($29,858,000).
F/N
There have been no exports of crude
petroleum to the Soviet Union in 1940, in
1939, or in 1938.

=== Page 359 === (confidence 0.99)
STATE
POLITICAL RELATIONS
-2-
4. Exports of lubricating oils to
Japan have decreased steadily from January
through March of this year. The total
for the first quarter of 1940 ($1,557,337)
is approximately one quarter of the 1939
total ($5,184,000) which was nearly twice
the 1938 totai ($2,789,000).
Exports of lubricating oil to the
Soviet Union have been negligible and declin-
ing in amounts in January through March of
this year. The total for the first quarter
of 1940 ($293) is more than the total for
the full year 1939 ($94) and that for 1938
($115).
COMMENT
The above figures suggest that exports
of petroleum products to Japan are of
steadily decreasing importance but still
remain significant in total amounts, requir-
ing the expenditure of considerable amounts
of foreign exchange. At the same time,
exports of petroleum products to the Soviet
Union which also appear to be declining
in importance do not appear to be of
much significance in their total amounts.

=== Page 365 === (confidence 0.99)
THE TUCSON CLINIC
110 South Scott Street
Tuoson, Arizona
May 20, 1940.
The Honorable Henry F. Ashurst
Senator for the State of Arizona
The United States Senate
Washington, D. C.
My dear Senator Ashurst:
I am writ ing to ask you if you will not investigate the
trade situation wh ich exists between the United States and
Japan to ascertain whether or not we are sell ing Japan the
very materials to enable her to become a real antagonist
of ours in the event we should become involved in a war with
that country. Also to see if it is not the materials we
are selling Japan which is enabling her to carry on her wanton
destruction of China.
From all I have been able to learn I believe this is just what
is taking place, and if this is true, it seems to me it is
time for the Congress of the United States to declare an
embargo on war materials destined for Japan.
I hope, if you find on investigation that the above is a
matter of fact, that this plan of embargo will have your
full suport.
I am
Very truly yours,
Sam'l H. Watson, M. D.
SHW/c

=== Page 379 === (confidence 0.99)
CORNELL & COMPANY
622-623-624 Rorabaugh-Wiley Bldg.
Hutchinson, Kansas
May 20, 1940
Mr. Clifford R. Hope,
United States Representative,
Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. Hope:
In Re: National Defense.
I wish to state that I favor adequate national defense
for the purpose of protecting this country against the
agression of any foreign power.
What 1s adequate defense is a question whioh, I
believe, must be entrusted to experts without policical
bias.
But why, I ask, does our government continue to
commit the same stupid blunder which England committed
a decade ago when she indirectly permitted her citizens
to aid and abet Hitler in building up his military menace
to the world.
I refer, of course, to our stupidity in continuing
to furnish Japan gasoline, oil, scrap iron, ammunition,
and 90% of all other supplies she needs in prosecuting
her war in China.
I believe any thinking person who investigates will
conclude that if Japan succeeds in what she has started
out to do in China, we will have a military menace to the
peace and safety of the world to our West, as well as to
our East. Is not one such menace enough?
When will our Congress enact laws that will put a
stop to this aiding and abetting this additional military
menace?
Yours truly,
HARRY FARIS
HF:B

EASTERN AFFAIRS
Dear Mr. Secretary:
A
I have pending before my Committee several resolutions
calling for an embargo on the exportation of scrap
materials to Japan Aside from the requests of Members
for hearings, I have received thousands of letters urging
action on the reso utions and in the past week they have
increased in considerable numbers.
Believe me that I do not wish to embarrass the Administra-
tion, but in view of the present situation I feel it an
opportune time to act and that it would meet with the
nation-wide approval of the American people. We have a
splendid reason for taking that position now in view of
our defense plans in that we will have need for scrap
materials in the immediate future. I recall the short-
age at the time of our entrance in the World War which
JUN 1 3 1940
is nothing to compare with the existing increased use.
FILED
I feel it my duty to call the matter to your attention
and I shall appreciate hearing from you.
Aaha
Sincerely yours,
MR.
JUN 8-194
F/N

Upon the receipt of the attached memorandum of May 9,
M
from the Division of Foreign Trade Statistics of the Bureau
89
of Foreign and Domestic Commerce, showing April exports of
4.
24/9
commodities subject to the so-called moral embargo to Japan
the Soviet Union, and Mexico, I telephoned Mr. Barton and
asked him to obtain further information in regard to the
941
listed shipments of 194,987 1bs. of wolfram ore and 7,003
1bs. of quicksilver to Japan. (Scrap rubber is not covered
by the moral embargo and I therefore made no inquiry in re-
gard to the large scrap rubber shipment listed.)
Some days later, Mr. Barton called me back and informed
me that both the wolfram ore and the quicksilver in question
had been exported by Japanese companies in the United States.
FILED

making the moral embargo effective in conneotion with shipments
